--- Page 128 ---
8. Liability

8.1 Reverse charges

The VAT Law stipulates that the reverse charge is applicable in the following five cases (Article 45

paragraph 1 of the VAT Law);
services performed by a non-resident taxable person not registered for VAT purposes in Switzerland,
provided that according to Article 8 paragraph 1 of the VAT Law the place of supply is in
Switzerland. However, the reverse charge is not applicable, if the non-resident taxable person renders
telecommunication services or clectronic services to non-taxable persons;

+ — import of data storage media without market value with all the services and rights included;

+ supplies of goods connected with immovable property by a non-resident taxable person not registered for
VAT purposes in Switzerland, provided that the supplies are not subject to import VAT;

+ supplies of electricity, natural gas or district heating by a non-resident taxable person to a taxable person
established in Switzerland; and

* transfer of emission rights and the like (as of 1 January 2025).

There is no obligation for the non-resident invoicing party to mention on the invoice that the reverse charge is
applicable.

8.1.1 Reverse charge mechanism - supply of services

‘The provision of a service does quality as a supply of services according to Article 8 paragraph 1 (basic rule)
as far as the VAT Law does not stipulate a specific rule regarding the place of supply. The services in question
are deemed to be performed where the recipient has established his business or has a fixed establishment for
which the services are supplied, or, in the absence of such a place of business or fixed establishment, the place
where he has his permanent address or usually resides. The basic rule does not make a difference between
B2B services and B2C services. Therefore, the provision of such services to a Swiss taxable person or a Swiss
resident will be subject to Swiss VAT.

In case the non-resident supplier is registered for VAT purposes in Switzerland, the invoice must be issued
with Swiss VAT. This applies even if the non-resident supplier is not established in Switzerland or the non-
resident supplier’s fixed establishment in Switzerland does not intervene in a supply.

In case the non-resident supplier is not registered for VAT purposes in Switzerland, the invoice must be issued
without Swiss VAT. Consequently, the customer subject to VAT must declare the VAT in its VAT return, In
general, the declared VAT can be deducted in the same VAT return (see also chapter 9).

‘The reverse charge mechanism also applies in case the customer is a non-taxable person in Switzerland,

e.g. a natural person or legal person who does not perform economic activities. However, in those cases the
customer is only liable for VAT if the services purchased amount to CHF 10,000 (approximately EUR 10,500)
‘or more in a calendar year. The VAT liability is limited to the purchases in question. The non-taxable person is
not entitled to deduct the input VAT due.

8.1.2 General reverse charge mechanism - supply of goods

Regarding the supply of goods, the reverse charge mechanism applies in case (see also chapter 3.2):
» the supplier is established or resident abroad;

+ the supplier is not registered for VAT purposes in Switzerland;

+ the supplies are not subject to import VAT; and

+ the supplies are connected with immovable property.

With regard to the tax liability, the same rules apply as for the supply of services. The competent authorities
do not have to inform the non-taxable person (c.g. a natural person without economic activities) anymore
about the tax liability.

For more information regarding the supply of goods with installation, see also chapter 3.2.

628 | Switzerland

--- Page 129 ---
8.1.3 Reverse charge mechanism - supply of natural gase lectricity and district heating

According to Article 7 paragraph 2 of the VAT Law, the place of a supply of electricity, natural gas or district
heating is deemed to be the place where the customer has established his business or has a fixed establishment
for which the goods are supplied, or, in the absence of such a place of business or fixed establishment, the place
where the electricity, the natural gas or the district heating is effectively used respectively consumed.
Regarding the place of supply, the VAT Law does not differentiate between the electricity, natural gas or district
heating supplied to a taxable person or a dealer and that supply to a non-taxable person or final consumer.

In case a non-resident taxable person supplies electricity, natural gas or district heating to a customer domiciled
in Switzerland, the reverse charge mechanism does apply if the customer is a taxable person. If the customer is

anon-taxable person, the reverse charge mechanism does not apply. Thus, the non-resident taxable person must
VAT register in Switzerland and issue an invoice with Swiss VAT (cf. chapter 1.1 and 1.2).

8.2 4i nt and several liability

According to the Swiss VAT Code the following are jointly and severally liable with the taxable person:

+ the partners in an ordinary partnership, general partnership or limited partnership within the scope of their
liability under civil law:

* persons who carry out a voluntary auction or have one carried out;

+ any person or partnership belonging to a VAT group (Art. 13), with the exception of pension funds, for
all taxes owed by the group; ifa person or partnership leaves the group, it is only liable for the tax claims
arising from its own business activities;

+ on the transfer of a company: the previous tax debtor for a period of three years from the notification or
termination of the transfer for tax claims arising prior to the transfer;

+ on termination of the tax liability of a dissolved legal entity, trading company or partnership without legal
personality: the persons entrusted with the liquidation up to the amount of the liquidation result; and

+ for the tax of a legal entity that transfers its registered office abroad: the managing bodies up to the
amount of the legal entity’s net assets.

a Recovery of input VAT

In principle, the taxable person has the right to deduct any input VAT provided that the purchased goods/
services are connected with his business activity. The VAT Law lists three categories of input VAT that can be
deducted by the taxable person in his VAT return (Article 28 paragraph | of the VAT Law):

+ VAT invoiced by a third party:

+ VAT declared in the VAT return in connection with the reverse charge procedure; and

+ import VAT paid/due as well as the declared import VAT in case the taxable person applied for the
postponement procedure.

Moreover, the taxable person must prove that the input VAT has been paid (Article 28 paragraph 4 of the VAT
Law). For this purpose, an invoice with all the relevant information (see also chapter 10.1.1) must be held by
the taxable person. However, it is also possible to prove by other means that the input VAT has been paid.

9.1 Non-recoverable input VAT

In principle, any input VAT is recoverable, i.e. the VAT Law does not contain any restrictions for certain categories
of input VAT. However, if the purchased goods/services are connected with a business activity exempt from VAT
according to Article 21 of the VAT Law, the taxable person is not entitled to deduct the input VAT (see chapter 7.3).

‘The input VAT on gifts is deductible, if the gifts are for business purposes. Provided that the value of the gifts

does not exceed the amount of CHF 500 (approximately EUR 525) per capita and per annum, the VAT Law
assumes that the gifts are for business purposes.

Switzerland | 629

--- Page 130 ---
9.2 Excess input VAT

Ifthe VAT return shows a credit position in favor of the taxable person, the FTA will remit the surplus amount
to balance the VAT account. A transfer into a foreign account is possible,

The VAT Law stipulates that the credit transfer must be done within 60 days after the receipt of the VAT
return, Once this period has expired, an interest for late payment is due by the FTA.

9.3 Capital goods - adjustment periods

+ immovable property - 20 years following the year of first use
+ movable property - 5 years following the year of first use
+ services - 5 years following the year of first use

However, in the latter case no adjustment takes place, if in the meantime the services have already been
consumed (e.g. advisory services that are deemed to have been consumed as soon as they have been received).

9.4 VAT refund for foreigners

Foreign taxable persons having received services subject to taxation (for example building of a booth, hotel
accommodation, car rent) are eligible for a refund of the Swiss VAT under specific pre-conditions. For this
purpose, a tax representative domiciled in Switzerland must be appointed.

In order to obtain a refund of the VAT, the following conditions must be met:

+ the applicant is a resident / domiciled abroad;

+ the applicant is not registered for VAT purposes in Switzerland or Liechtenstein (no tax liability according
to Article 10 of the VAT Law);

+ the applicant does not perform any taxable supplies in Switzerland;

+ proof issued by the competent Tax Authorities that the applicant is VAT registered in the country where he
is resident / domiciled (a certificate of the business status);

> the invoices submitted must meet the formal requirements according to Article 26 Paragraph 2 of the VAT
Law and must correspond with the refund period; and

+ the country where the applicant is resident / domiciled is granting an equivalent reciprocal right to Swiss
entrepreneurs (see Attps.//tinyurl.com/ms8cd7xy (Landerliste].

Only one application can be submitted per year. No VAT refund will be granted for a total amount of less than
CHF 500 (approximately EUR 525). The application for a VAT refund must be submitted with the official
forms approved by the FTA (forms no. 1222 and 1223).

The application for a VAT refund must be submitted between 1 January and 30 June of the following calendar
year. This 30 June deadline cannot be extended.

The official forms must be submitted together with the original invoices and original proof issued by the
competent Tax Authorities that the applicant is VAT registered, to this mailing address:

Eide. Steuerverwaltung
Hauptabteilung Mehrwertsteuer
Schwarztorstrasse 50

CH - 3003 Bern

Switzerland

There is no time limit for making a decision. However, in case the VAT refund is granted later than 180 days
after the application for a refund is submitted, late interest is due from the Tax Authorities. In general, no

formal decision is taken by the Tax Authorities. Thus, there is no specific time limit for an “appeal”.

In regard to restrictions on recoverability of the VAT, please refer to chapter 9.1.

10. Administrative obligations

10.1 Invoicing

10.1.1 Invoice content

Under the current VAT legislation there is a legal definition for the term “invoice” in Article 3 lit. k of the VAT

Law that reads as follows:

* an invoice is “any document with which settlement is made for a supply with a third party, irrespective of
how the document is described in business transactions”.

‘The invoice must clearly identify the supplier, the recipient and the nature of the supply and, as a rule, has to

contain the following elements:

+ the name and the location of the supplier, in the form in which it presents itself in business transactions,
and the number under which it is entered in the Register of Taxable Persons;

+» the name and location of the customer, in the form in which it presents itself in business transactions;

+ date or period of the rendering of the supply, to the extent it differs from the invoice date;

* nature, object and scope of the supply;

+ the consideration for the supply; and

+ the applicable tax rate and the tax amount payable on of the consideration; if the consideration includes
the tax, the quotation of the applicable tax rate suffices.

10.1.2 Time limits

‘The Swiss VAT rules do not provide a time limit before which an invoice must be issued. There is a legal
obligation for the supplier to issue an invoice upon the request of the customer. This invoice must conform to
the specifications already mentioned (see chapter 10.1.1).

10.1.3 Currency

Invoices as well as credit notes can be issued in any official currency. There is no obligation to put the VAT
amount in local currency, i.e. in Swiss Francs.

10.1.4 Conversion and exchange rate

Due to the fact that the VAT return has to be submitted in Swiss Francs, the amounts invoiced in foreign
currency must be converted into Swiss Francs.

For the conversion into Swiss Franes the official exchange rates must be applied, i.e. either the monthly
average exchange rate of the FTA or the daily currency (sale) exchange rate. There is no obligation to mention
the exchange rate applied on the invoice or credit note.

For the monthly average exchange rate see the following webpage: hifps://tinyurl.com/2wyw968a

For the daily currency (sale) exchange rate see the following webpage: https./vww.rates.bazg.admin.ch/home

10.1.5 Self-billing

Self-billing is understood as a situation where the customer issues the invoice instead of the supplier and
provides the supplier with a copy. This self-billing document is in the VAT Law treated as an “ordinary”
invoice issued by the supplier. The term used in the legal provisions for such a document is a “credit note”.
‘There are no specific conditions to be met if a self-billing arrangement is set up between the supplier and the
customer. In practice there is always an agreement between the parties in place.

Only in cases where the credit note shows VAT although the supplier is not entitled to charge VAT, or the VAT
amount charged is too high for the supply (e.g. the standard rate is applied instead of the reduced rate), the
recipient of the credit note (the supplier) must submit a written objection to the customer.

Otherwise, the VAT shown on the self-billing document is owed and must be paid to the PTA by the supplier.
Switzerland | 631

--- Page 132 ---
10.1.6 Simplified invoice

A simplified invoice (receipt) must contain the following information:

+ the name and the location of the supplier, in the form in which it presents itself in business transactions,
and the number, under which it is entered in the Register of Taxable Persons;

+ date or period of the rendcring of the supply, to the extent it differs from the invoice date;

* nature, object and scope of the supply;

+ the consideration for the supply;

+ the applicable tax rate and the tax amount payable on of the consideration; and

+ ifthe consideration includes the tax, the quotation of the applicable tax rate suffices.

It is possible to issue a simplified invoice if the amount of the invoice is not higher than CHF 400
(approximately EUR 420), However, such simplified invoices do not entitle the foreign recipient to a VAT
refund (cf. chapter 9.4).

10.1.7 Storage of invoices

The taxable person must retain in a proper manner its books of account, vouchers, business documents and
other records until the absolute time limitation of the tax claim (which is 10 years after the end of the tax period
in which the tax claim arose (Article 42 paragraph 6 of the VAT Law). Business documents that are required

in connection with the calculation of de-taxation (i.e. if the conditions for the input tax deduction arise later,

the input tax can be deducted in the reporting period in which the conditions arose) and own use of immovable
goods, are to be retained for 20 years (Article 31 paragraph 3 and Article 32 paragraph 2 of the VAT Law).

The storage obligation is based on Article 957 of the Swiss Civil Law. It requires that the income statement
and the balance sheet must be signed and kept in paper form. Electronic storage is permitted, provided that
the legibility is guaranteed at all times (see also chapter 10.1.8). It is mandatory to archive electronically
the electronic import and export declarations issued by the Federal Customs Administration (FCA), as these
contain an electronic signature. In a release dated 20 September 2011, the FCA declared that the printing
on paper of an electronic import or export declaration has, in principle, a lower probative value than the
electronically stored original

10.1.8 Electronic invoicing

Data that are electronically transmitted and stored in a comparable manner and information that is relevant for
the deduction, the tax collection or tax reference, have the same probative value as the data and information
that are readable without aids, provided, the following conditions are met:

+ proof of origin;

* demonstration of integrity; and

+ non-repudiation of sending.

The availability of the data and information stored electronically or in a comparable manner relevant for the
tax collection or tax reference, is governed by the provisions of the third section of the regulation of 24 April
2002 on account books (SR 221,431). The taxable person must ensure that this data and information can

be made readable at any time during the statutory retention period. The taxable person must provide for the
necessary resources.

Documents can be transmitted electronically to the FTA, if the FTA has expressly declared this form of
transmission permissible (cf. Article 70 para. 4 of the VAT Law and Article 122 MWSTV).

10.2 VAT compliance

10.2.1 VAT return period

Asa general rule, the VAT returns are to be filed quarterly.
In case the taxable person applied for the simplitied accounting method (so-called Saldosteuersatzmethode),
the VAT returns have to be filed half-yearly.

In case the taxable person is regularly in a credit position, an application can be submitted for the filing of
monthly VAT returns.

632 | Switzerland

--- Page 133 ---
As of | January 2025, the taxable person can apply for filing yearly VAT returns provided that the annual
turnover does not exceed CHF 5,005,000 (approximately EUR 5,250,000). The authorization will not be
granted or revoked, if the taxable persons cease to comply with the settlement and payment obligations,
10.2.2 Filing of a VAT return and payment

Since 2015, it is possible to submit the VAT returns electronically, The filing in paper form is only permitted
in exceptional cases. For each VAT retum in paper form, a new application has to be submitted.
The taxable person is obliged to file the VAT return within 60 days after the end of the reporting period.

The VAT due must be remitted within the same period to the bank account of the FTA (e.g. the VAT due for
the first quarter 2025 must be credited to the account of the FTA by 30 May 2025).

If the taxable person files yearly VAT returns, provisional tax payments will be assessed and invoiced by the
FTA.

10.2.3 European sales listing

Because Switzerland is not a member of the EU, this requirement is not applicable.

10.2.4 National recapitulative statement
There is no national recapitulative statement required.

10.2.5 Tax assessment period

‘The VAT Law stipulates that the VAT is levied per tax period. In general, the tax period is the calendar year.
Thus, in case of a tax audit, the tax assessment must cover one full year.

In principle, the FTA can arrange for a tax audit any time as long as the tax claim is not yet prescribed,
In Switzerland, the prescription period is 5 years after the end of the tax period in which the tax claim is
incurred. This means that in the year 2025, the last open year for a tax assessment is the year 2020.

10.3 Penalties

10.3.1 Default penalties for late registration

‘The period for filing the online questionnaire is 30 days from the time when the tax liability begins. In case of
late registration, the FTA can assess a fine of up to CHF 10,000 (approximately EUR 10,500).

10.3.2 Default penalties for not filing a VAT return or a European sales listing on time

The period for filing the quarterly VAT return is 60 days. It is identical with the payment deadline. If no or only
incomplete records are made available to the FTA or if the results reported obviously do not match the actual
facts, the FTA is eligible to assess the tax claim due. The tax claim is established with an assessment notice.
‘There is no such obligation with respect to filing a European sales listing, as Switzerland is not a Member
State of the EU.

10.3.3 Default penalties for not paying VAT on time to the tax authorities

Within 60 days after the end of the reporting period, the taxable person must settle the tax claim that arose
in the respective period. If the taxable person makes no payment or a payment that is obviously insufficient,
the FTA can, after issuing a reminder, seek legal enforcement of the tax amount provisionally payable for the
reporting period in question.

If there is no return for the taxable person or a return that is obviously inadequate, the FTA first determines
the tax amount provisionally payable (see also chapter 10.2.2).

10.3.4 Default penalties on VAT assessments when VAT is not reported correctly

For tax evasion a fine of up to CHF 400,000 (approximately EUR 420,000) is imposed on anyone who

intentionally or negligently reduces the tax claim to the detriment of the state, by:

* not declaring ina tax period all receipts, declaring too high receipts from supplies exempt from the tax,
not declaring all expenses subject to acquisition tax or by declaring too high expenses entitling to input
tax deduction;

Switzerland | 633

--- Page 134 ---
+ by claiming an incorrect refund; or
+ by claiming an unjustified tax abatement.

The amount of the fine is up to CHF 800,000 (approximately EUR 840,000), if the tax evaded in the cases
mentioned above entitles the invoice recipient to input tax deduction.

A fine of up to CHF 200,000 (approximately EUR 210,000) is imposed on anyone who reduces the tax claim
to the detriment of the state by declaring the tax factors relevant for establishment of the tax truthfully, but
qualifies them incorrectly for tax purposes, provided that he intentionally does not apply properly clear legal
provisions, ordinances of the authorities or published practice rules and does not inform the authorities, in
writing, in advance. If perpetrated negligently, the fine is up to CHF 20,000 (approximately EUR 21,000).

A fine of up to CHF 800,000 (approximately EUR 840,000) is imposed on anyone who reduces the tax claim
to the detriment of the state, by:

+ intentionally or negligently not declaring or declaring incorrectly or concealing goods on import; or
+ in response to an enquiry, intentionally giving no, false or incomplete information during an official audit
or administrative procedure that has, as its object, the establishment of the tax claim or tax abatement.

If the tax benefit is obtained on the basis of an incorrect return, the tax evasion is not punishable before the
period for correction of errors in the return has expired (Article 72 paragraph 1 of the VAT Law) while the
error still has not been corrected.

10.4 Intrastat

There is no such obligation as Switzerland is nol a Member State of the EU.

The United Kingdom

Since leaving the European Union on 31 December 2020 the principal VAT legislation in the United Kingdom
(UK) is the Value Added Tax Law 1994. This is supplemented by a number of Statutory Instruments,
primarily the Value Added Tax Regulations 1995, VAT is referred to in the UK as “Value Added Tax” or
“VAT”.

Legislation as to penalties and the powers of the tax authority, HM Revenue & Customs (hereafter HMRC), is
contained within separate Finance Laws.

Some further UK VAT Law sits in tertiary legislation. This is delegated legislation which sits in Public
Notices published by HMRC. The most important pieces of tertiary legislation sit in the following documents:

+ Public Notice 700, Chapter 19 which sets out the UK’s record keeping requirements;

* Public Notices 702 & 703 which cover the scope and conditions for VAT relief on UK imports and
exports respectively,

+ Notice 708 includes certificates that are required before VAT relief can be applied to certain property
related supplies which is a very complex area of UK VAT legislation, and

+ Public Notice 742A, which sets out the requirements for applying the option to tax on land and property
transactions.

‘Text which has the force of law tends to sit within a text box in the Public Notices headed “The following
statement has the force of law”.

New rules were introduced by the Northern Ireland Protocol effective from January 2021 which dealt

with VAT accounting on EU transactions involving Northern Ireland (NI). At the time of going to print

the UK rules affecting NI are subject to changes are being rolled out following conclusion of the Windsor
Framework, which amended the original NI Protocol by introducing a new UK internal market system

(aka a ‘green lane’). Rules outlined in this chapter relating to supplies of goods from or to the UK should
generally be read as excluding those with NI. The current position for supplies involving NI are summarised
in paragraph 10.5.5.

i Taxable persons

11 Registration threshold for domestic taxable persons

For UK established businesses the current UK VAT threshold is GBP 90,000 (approximately EUR 106,250)

per year. The registration threshold is reviewed annually during the approval of the UK Budget (normally in

March each year). There are two ways in which this threshold can be exceeded:

+ Past turnover: if'a UK person’s taxable turnover exceeded GBP 90,000 (approximately EUR 106,250)
within the prior 12 months or less e.g. ifa UK taxable person’s turnover exceeds the threshold in the prior
7 months, it must notify HMRC by the end of the 8* month and start charging VAT from the beginning of
the 9" month.

+ Future turnover: if a UK taxable person reasonably expects to exceed the threshold in the next 30 days
alone. In this case, it must register for VAT immediately and start charging VAT from the day it realises
that it will exceed the threshold in the next 30 days.

It is also possible for a UK established business to register for VAT without exceeding the threshold. This is
known as a ‘voluntary registration’ and as long as a UK taxable person is making taxable supplies, or can
prove that he intends to make taxable supplies, it can register for VAT voluntarily. This benefits by allowing
for reclaims of VAT on costs being incurred in advance of making taxable supplies.

‘The United Kingdom | 635

--- Page 136 ---
The registration process must now use HMRC’s online registration system. Unless the application is being
submitted using a UK VAT Agent’s own Agency Services Access, the applicant must first register to use
HMRC online services and the link is best found using the Google search term “Welcome to HMRC online
services”. Having registered for online services (it is important to make a note of the Government Gateway
TD as it will be needed again during the process), there are then clear links to registering for UK VAT.

The registration application needs to be supported by evidence of ID for the applicant and also that trading
has either already begun or will later take place. The online registration procedure is intended for use by non-
VAT specialists and often seeks to gather and call for supplementary information (depending upon the nature
of the supplies made or to be made) to be uploaded during the registration process. Despite this, HMRC is
likely to contact an applicant for additional information, especially where registrations involve either land and
property or a business which has not yet commenced trading. HMRC also frequently reject applications for
non-established businesses which can be frustrating, leading to further applications needing to be made.

New registration applications can take 8 weeks or more to be processed, however much longer processing
periods can arise especially where HMRC query or reject the application. It is not possible to contact HMRC
to query any application until after 8 weeks has passed.

1.2 Registration threshold for foreign taxable persons

The UK VAT registration threshold for non-established taxable persons is GBP nil, i.e. no threshold applies
and the non-established businesses making any supplies of taxable goods or services that are treated as being
made in the UK and are not subject to the reverse charge, MUST register and account for UK VAT on the
sales regardless of the level of turnover.

When registering for UK VAT, in addition to the on-line application form being fully completed the following
information must also be provided:

+ Form VAT 2 which is needed if the application is for a partnership;

+ Form 5L which is needed if the business is involved in real estate;

+ Where an agent is being used then a form 64-8 is needed to evidence the agent’s authority to act on the
applicant's behalf, and

* Proof of ID and home address for the person signing the application.

Foreign taxable persons without fixed establishments in the UK can choose to either register:

+ direct with HMRC and have the registration recorded against the HMRC Non-Established Taxable
Persons Unit (NETPU); Ruby House, 8 Ruby Place, Aberdeen AB10 1ZP; or

+ by appointing a UK VAT Agent who can streamline the application and help avoid postal delays.

HMRC may require Non-UK applicants to appoint a tax representative who is jointly and severally liable for
any tax, interest or penalties due.

Further information on registering for VAT can be found at this link: htqps://www.gov.uk/vat-registration

13 VAT grouping
VAT grouping is not compulsory in the UK.
‘Two or more companies or limited liability partnerships - known as ‘corporate bodies’ and non corporate

bodies with close financial, economic and organizational links may register as a single taxable person, or VAT
group, if they meet the following criteria:

+ each body has its principal or registered office in the UK; and
+ they are under common control, e.g. one or more companies are a subsidiary of a parent company.

Non-resident companies can apply to be members of a UK VAT group provided that they meet the above
conditions and have a fixed establishment in the UK.

636 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 137 ---
If the turnover of a group of companies is over GBP 10,000,000 (approximately EUR 11,803,700 at July
2024) per year and the group is partly owned or managed by a third party, it can only register as a group for
VAT ift

+ no more than 50% of benefits generated by the business go to third parties;
* the group uses consolidated accounting; and
+ no third party consolidates the group into its accounts,

A VAT group is treated in the same way as a single company registered for VAT on its own. The registration

is made in the name of the ‘representative member’, The representative member is responsible for completing
and submitting a single VAT return and making VAT payments or receiving VAT refunds on behalf of the VAT
group. A single VAT identification number is issued for the whole VAT group, but each group member has a
unique identifier, No VAT is accounted for on supplies between VAT group members.

All the group companies are jointly and severally liable for any VAT debts.

In addition to completing an on-line application to register form for the new VAT group, VAT forms 50 and
VAT 51 also must be completed. More information as to VAT grouping, as well as access to the relevant forms
can be found at the following link: hitps://tinyurl.com/mtpa9pbk Companies without economic activity can be
a member of a UK VAT group. Holding companies can be members of a VAT group and are often selected as,
but do not need to act as the VAT group’s representative member.

14 Fiscal representative

ANon-Established Taxable Person (NETP) can register for VAT in the UK in one of the three ways:

+ directly if they can meet the UK record and inspection requirements;

+ through a standard VAT registration application using a UK VAT Agent; or

* through a standard VAT registration application using a fiscal representative, also known as a tax
representative.

HMRC can direct that certain NETPs appoint a fit and proper UK tax representative, normally where HMRC
believes that there is a risk to the revenue. NETPs, based in countries where certain mutual assistance
arrangements exist, cannot be forced to have a tax representative. HMRC normally expect the appointment
ofa UK tax representative using form VATITR if there is any distance selling into Northern Ireland (see
paragraph 10.5.5).

A foreign taxable person does not have to appoint a fiscal representative in order to qualify for certain tax
schemes and simplifications (e.g. for use of the Postponed VAT Accounting for imports of goods into the UK).

Any UK person, whether natural person or otherwise; that is unconnected with the business, or key persons
involved in that business and considered to meet or exceed a particular level of suitability, compliance and

integrity; can act as a fiscal representative provided they accept the associated joint and several liability.
2. Taxable transactions
21 Transfer of business

Asa general rule, the sale of the assets of a business including goodwill will be subject to VAT. However a
mandatory VAT relief can apply to the transfer of a business as a going concern (TOGC) so that the proceeds
fall outside the scope of UK VAT such that VAT is not chargeable. It is important to be aware that the UK.
TOGC rules are not optional. For the relief to apply the arrangements surrounding the sale and those adopted
by the parties must meet certain conditions which HMRC sees as being:

The United Kingdom i 637

--- Page 138 ---
+ the assets must be sold as part of the transfer of'a ‘business’ as a “going concern’;

+ the assets are to be used by the transferee with the intention of carrying on the same kind of ‘business’ as
the transferor (but not necessarily identical);

+ where the transferor is a taxable person, the transferee must be a taxable person already or become one as
the result of the transfer;

+ in respect of land which would be standard rated if it were supplied, the transferee must notify HMRC
that he has opted to tax the land by the relevant date, and must notify the transferor by the same date that
its option has not been disapplied (NB. these rules are very complicated);

+ where only part of the ‘business’ is sold, it must be capable of operating separate!

+ there must not be a series of immediately consecutive ‘transtérs of business’.

ind

2.2 Supplies free of charge

In principle where goods or services are provided free of charge HMRC consider that there has been no

business activity and therefore that input tax incurred on providing the free goods or services cannot be

reclaimed or, if input VAT has been reclaimed, that output VAT falls due on the free disposal as though a sale

had taken place. This includes free use of business assets which leads to a deemed supply on which output

VAT falls due, including use of fuel for private purposes. Business prometon schemes can also lead to deemed

supplies with output VAT due despite no monetary payment being made by the recipient. However there are

some exceptions including:

+ where the total cost of all business gifts you make to the same person does not exceed GBP 50, excluding
VAT, in any {2-month period no VAT adjustment is needed;

+ HMRC also accept that providing samples of products free of charge to individuals for marketing
purposes is not liable to VAT adjustment; and

+ VAT also does not need to be adjusted on staff entertainment provided the employees are not acting as
hosts to non-employees.

2.3. Importation

2.3.1 Importation followed by intra-Community supply

‘As the UK is no longer a member of the EU there cannot be any intra-Community supplies made (except to
the extent that transactions involve NI - see paragraph 10.5.5). However, subject to a number of conditions, it
is possible for a UK VAT registered importer to avoid a VAT charge on UK imports where the goods are to be
shipped immediately either to an EU Member State or another country.

2.3.1.1 Postponed VAT Accounting on importation

From January 2021, UK importers who are registered for UK VAT have a choice about how they handle their
import VAT liabilities. This can either be via the long-standing import VAT system that requires payment of
VAT to HMRC at the point of UK entry or use the new Postponed VAT Accounting (PVA) system (outlined
below). In order to use PVA and handle import VAT via entries on a VAT Return, the importer must inform the
person dealing with the customs entry (the import agent) in writing to select the PVA option as the method of
payment on the customs declaration and enter the importer’s details as the consignee.

The benefit of PVA is that the importer does not have to make cash payments of import VAT but instead

records both the import VAT due and the corresponding reclaim on the same UK VAT return covering the date

of import. To make a reclaim the importer will either need:

= to obtain and retain a monthly PVA Statement from HMRC’s online Customs Declaration Service (CDS); or

~ where PVA is not specified on the entry declaration, the importer will be required to pay the VAT over to
HMRC and any later VAT claim will need to be supported by a C79, which is a statement produced each
month by HMRC for non-PVA imports declared against each importer’s EORI.

PVA cannot be used to postpone payment of import or other duties, which remain due at the point of import
entry unless deferred as outlined below.

Per paragraph 3.3 new rules apply from January 2021 to import consignments by ecommerce sellers and
online marketplaces (OMP) that do not exceed GBP 135. Instead of paying import VAT the seller or OMP
must be VAT registered and charge VAT at the point of sale.

638 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 139 ---
2.3.1.2 Duty Deferment (‘DD’)

Although negative for cash-flow when compared to PVA importers, including non-established businesses,
can apply to use duty deferment, which is a system in operation prior to Brexit that can still be used if PVA
is not selected on the import entry. In some cases HMRC will require that the importer provides a financial
guarantee from a HMRC approved bank, insurance company or building society to provide support behind
the duty deferment account. The system is used to defer payment not only of VAT, but also customs duties,
excise duties, CAP levies, monetary compensatory amounts under the CAP, anti-dumping or countervailing
duties imposed by the BU, compensatory interest on IPR goods diverted to free circulation, and interest
charges on customs debts, excise duty, VAT, tobacco products duty and post clearance demands.

Import VAT and duties can also be deferred by using an agent’s DD account. In any case all payments are
deferred until the 15" day of the following month. Payment must be made to HMRC by the BACS (Bankers”
Automated Clearing Services) system of direct debit upon a UK bank account in GBP. BACS is a UK scheme
for the electronic processing of financial transactions.

3. Place of Supply

Pre-Brexit Simplification measures

While the UK was a member of the EU there were a number of simplification measures in operation. Since 01
January 2021 these measures have been largely removed (but see also paragraph 10.5.5 re the NI Protocol and
the Windsor Framework), The new rules now operating under each of these former measures are set out in the
following headings.

3.1 Consignment and call-off stock

Ifa non-UK supplier holds any stocks of goods in the UK, even if these are at a customer’s warehouse for
their call-off, the arrival of goods into the UK will have to be treated as an import. Given that ownership of
the goods remains with the non-UK supplier, that same ‘person’ will be liable for payment of any import VAT
and duty.

When ownership of the goods is transferred from the non-UK supplier to the UK customer, the place of the
supply is treated as being in the UK and therefore (with nil VAT registration threshold applying), the supplier
must register for UK VAT and charge UK VAT at the appropriate rate. Once UK VAT registered the non-UK
supplier would become able to take advantage of the PVA Scheme -s ee paragraph 2.3.2.1.

There is also no simplification for consignment stocks. These are treated as a transfer of own goods for VAT.
that will also necessitate UK VAT registration of the supplier.

3.2. Installation supplies

‘There is no legal definition of ‘supply with installation or assembly’. HMRC’s guidance says that the “key
here is that a supplier, or an agent acting on its behalf, is required (as part of the supply) to install or assemble
the goods for the customer”. Importantly, HMRC sees that, for place of supply purposes, having separate
contracts for the goods and for the installation or assembly should still be seen as a single supply of installed
or assembled goods. In all such situations, the place of supply is where the installation or assembly is carried
out which, if located in the UK, would ordinarily result in the supplier having to register for UK VAT.

However, for non-UK suppliers it may be possible to avoid having to register for UK VAT using a limited
simplification for installed or assembled goods imported from a third country under which any VAT otherwise
due on the supply is handled by the customer self-accounting using the reverse charge for the supplies
received from its EU or non-EU suppliers.

When the overseas supplier does not have a UK establishment, it may or may not be required to register and
account for VAT depending on the VAT registration status of its customer. VAT registration will always be
required where the installation is in the UK and the customer is not registered for UK VAT.

The United Kingdom | 639

--- Page 140 ---
The simplification only applies where the supply is a one-off supply and allows the supplier to exceptionally
treat the supply as taking place outside the UK. Where this condition is met, the customer acts as the importer
of the goods and the full contract price must be declared on the import entry. While saving the supplier from
having to have a UK VAT registration, this approach also has the effect of increasing the value on which
import duty have to be charged, over and ahove the value that would be used for a simple importation of the
goods.

The simplification cannot be applied if an overseas supplier has a fixed establishment in the UK, in which
case it will need to register and account for VAT subject to the normal registration rules.

Where the simplification is used and UK VAT is incurred on goods and services purchased in the UK,
including small quantities of materials such as nuts and bolts or welding rods, this might be reclaimed via a
mechanism that is similar to the 13" Directive VAT refund procedure. Details can be found in HMRC public
guidance VAT Notice 723A.

A simplified procedure is available for EU businesses installing or assembling goods in Northern Ireland per
paragraph 10.5.5.

3.3 Distance sales

Except for distance sales to Northern Ireland (see paragraph 10.5.5) there is no VAT registration threshold for
non-UK e-commerce sellers, so they now need to register for UK VAT.

How UK VAT rules apply depends on whether the consignment value is above or below GBP 135. Where

consignment value is:

+ GBP 135 or below UK VAT has to be accounted on the supply at the point of sale, which means the
supplier has to either register for VAT or sell through an on-line market place and have VAT handled for
them; and

+ above GBP 135 then import VAT will be charged when the goods enter to UK. If the supplier acts as
importer of record then it will have to register for UK VAT and charge VAT on the onward sale to the UK
customer.

Ifa UK supplier makes distance sales in an EU Member State then a VAT registration may be required
depending on the value of the goods and whether the customer is prepared to act as importer.

3.4 Simplified Triangulation

Since leaving the BU, the UK is no longer eligible to use the triangulation simplification potentially leading
to the middleman having to register for VAT in the country of the arrival of the goods. This would be the case
for both a UK supplier having goods delivered into an EU state or an EU or non-EU supplier having goods
delivered to a UK customer.

AUK business can still use an EU VAT number in a triangular transaction but only if it involves parties who
belong in other BU states provided it does not involve goods moving from or to the UK. This is understood
to extend lo XI VAT ID numbers allocated to businesses making supplies from or to Norther Ireland (see
paragraph 10.5.5).

3.5 Acquisition threshold

As the UK is no longer a member of the EU there is no threshold for intra-Community acquisition of goods
by non-taxable persons.

3.6 Effective use and enjoyment

Use and enjoyment rules dictate the place of supply by looking at where the underlying services are
effectively used and enjoyed rather than where the supplier or customer belongs, The use and enjoyment
rules apply to telecommunications services and broadcasting services (B2B and B2C), electronically supplied
services (B2B), hired goods, hired means of transport and insurance repair services (B2B). While this rule can

640 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 141 ---
result in a supply being treated as made outside the scope of UK VAT, it can also result in a VAT registration
being required in the EU state where the supply is treated as taking place.

4. Chargeable event & Chargeability of VAT

4.1 Principles

In the case of a supply of goods taking place in the UK, in principle the VAT becomes chargeable at the time

the goods are removed or made available to the person to whom they are supplied. This is known as the basic
tax point. The basic tax point for services is when the performance of that service is completed, If an invoice

is issued within 14 days of the basic tax point the invoice date becomes the actual tax point.

4.2 Advance payments

For supplies of both goods and services a tax point is created by the receipt of payment so that VAT can
become chargeable before the time the goods are supplied or made available to the person to whom they

are being supplied or the service is completed or the invoice being issued. To the extent that any payment is
received in advance, an ‘actual’ tax point arises and VAT will fall due on the VAT return covering the date of
the payment receipt.

4.3 Continuous supplies

Where there is a continuous supply of services the time of supply is the earlier of when a payment is received,
ora VAT invoice is issued. Anti-avoidance rules also apply so that HMRC impose a tax point at the end of
each 12-month period.

44 Cash accounting

A Cash Accounting Scheme allows for taxable persons to account for output VAT on sales on the basis of
payments received rather than invoices issued. If this scheme is used, then input VAT incurred can only be
reclaimed once the supplier has been paid. The following conditions apply:

* to join the scheme the annual value of taxable supplies must not exceed GBP 1,350,000 (approximately
EUR 1,590,000);

+ all prior VAT returns must have been submitted within the statutory deadlines, and any amounts of VAT
due fully paid to HMRC;

+ the applicant must not have been assessed to a penalty for VAT evasion involving dishonest conduct in the
last year;

* or convicted of any VAT offence; and

+ they must leave the scheme at the end of the tax period in which their taxable turnover exceeds GBP
1,600,000 (approximately EUR 1,900,000).

5. Taxable amount

5.1 Bad debt relief

Acclaim for VAT Bad Debt Relief (‘BDR’) can be made on a UK VAT retum to the extent of the amount of
VAT outstanding on sales invoices that has been paid over to HMRC, that remains wholly or partly unpaid
after 6 months of the invoice due date and the obligation to pay is less than 4.5 years old. If any payment in
settlement is subsequently received then the BDR claim must be reversed. Claims cannot be made if the debt
has been sold or handed to a factoring company or if the supplier charged more than the normal selling price
for the supplies concemed.

In addition BDR applies to purchases so that where, 6 months after receiving a supply, a customer has not
paid for the supplies made to them, they must reverse all claims to input tax to the extent of the unpaid
amounts.

The United Kingdom | 641

--- Page 142 ---
Ifa taxable person wants to make a BDR claim and reclaim the VAT it has paid on unpaid sales, it must:

+ Hold a copy of the VAT invoice(s) on which it is claiming BDR. If it did not issue a VAT invoice, it must
keep paperwork showing the equivalent information; and
+ operate a separate bad debt account showing:
+ the amount written offas a bad debt;
+ the VAT on which it has claimed bad debt relicf;
+ the VAT period in which it claimed relief;
+ the total amount of VAT charged on each sale;
+ the VAT period in which it originally paid the VAT;
+ any payments received (shown separately for each invoice);
+ the name of the customer, and
+ the invoice date and number, or similar information, to which the debt relates. It needs to keep this
information for 4 years after it makes the claim.

5.2 Cash discounts

Irrespective of whether a discount is offered or adopted, suppliers must account for VAT on the amount they
actually receive and customers may only recover the amount of VAT that is actually paid to the supplier.
When a discount is offered and payment is made within the settlement period, the VAT due is based on the
discounted amount which can mean processing a credit note to evidence the VAT adjustment. To reduce VAT
due from the value shown on the sales invoice issued, the supplier is required to notify its customers of the
VAT discount available and the amounts the customer is due to pay. This can be done in one of two ways:

1. issue an invoice for the full net and VAT payable. If the invoice is paid within the settlement period, issue
a further VAT only credit note to account for the VAT discount; or

2. issue an invoice document which states the amount of discount (net and VAT) that can be claimed if

the invoice is paid within the settlement period as well as the full amount (net and VAT) due after the

settlement period. If the invoice is paid within the discount period there is no need lo issue an additional

credit note, bul accounting adjustments will need to be made.

- HMRC recommend that the invoice includes the statement “A discount of X% of the full price
applies if payment is made within Y days of the invoice date. No credit note will be issued. Following
payment you must ensure you have only recovered the VAT actually paid.”

- When an invoice is paid immediately, the settlement discount is automatically taken. The VAT is
already discounted on the invoice and no subsequent adjusting credit note is required.

53 Open market value

In certain circumstances, supplies must be valued at their ‘open market value’ for VAT purposes rather than in
accordance with the normal valuation principles of looking only at the consideration paid. Open market value
rules may apply where there are:

* — supplies between certain connected persons;

+ ‘direct selling’ arrangements;

+ a ‘self-supply’ occurs under rules for stock in trade cars; and

+ where fuel is supplied to employees / certain connected persons at below cost.

54 Returnable packaging

Ifa deposit is taken against the safe return of goods, it is not viewed as consideration for a supply so that no VAT
falls due. The contract should specify that the deposit is refundable subject to the safe return of the goods, so that
where the deposit is forfeited, either in total or in part, due to the fact that the customer failed to return the goods or
they were damaged, the payment then retained is outside the scope of VAT by being viewed as compensation. The
same VAT-free treatment can be applied, if'a business adds an extra charge to a supply of goods for the container
in order to ensure the safe retum of the container and commits to refund the charge when returned. However, if the
charge has been made to cover the hire, loan or use of the container, then the charge represents consideration for a
supply of services, even if it is refundable when the container is returned, so that VAT falls due on the change made.

642 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 143 ---

The standard rate in the UK is 20%.

7. Exemptions

7.1 Exemptions with right to deduction
In the UK our equivalent category to exemptions with right Lo deduction is our zero-rate as outlined above.

7.1.1 Intra-Community supplies

Except for transactions involving Northern Ireland (see paragraph 10.5.5) since leaving the EU, the UK
applies the same VAT treatment to EU transactions as it does to those with non-EU customers or suppliers.

7.1.2 Export supplies - Exports from the UK are strictly zero-rated but included here for ease of
indexing.

For the supply of goods which are exported, the following applies. Since January 2021 the same conditions

apply for zero rating all exports of goods from the UK. To qualify for zero-rating documentary evidence must

show that the goods have left the UK. Copies of transport documents alone will not be sufficient. The export

evidence must clearly identify:

+ the supplier:

+ the consignor (where different from the supplier);

+ the customer;

The official evidence is produced by the Customs systems used and will be dictated by the means of transit
and route used. It might also include a Single Administrative Document (SAD) endorsed by Customs at

Photocopy certificates of shipment are not normally acceptable as evidence of export, nor are photocopy bills
of lading, seaway bills or airway bills (unless authenticated with an original stamp and dated by an authorized
official of the issuing office).

7.2.1 Warehousing

7.2.1.1 VAT warehouse

There is no specific VAT warehousing regime in the UK. However, a customs warehouse, normally used for
customs duty purposes, can be also be used solely for VAT, if required. See below for details.

7.2.1.2 Excise warehouse

An excise warehouse must be authorised by HMRC to receive and store goods that are liable for excise duty
without having to pay the duty. The duty and VAT is suspended for the period that the goods are stored inside
the approved warehouse.

7.2.1.3 Bonded (customs) warehouse
It is possible to postpone paying VAT and duties by putting goods into a customs warehouse (often called
a ‘bonded warehouse’) which can either be a defined location such as a building (and be either public or
private) or an inventory system authorised by HMRC for storing non-UK goods (a ‘type E” warehouse).

A public warehouse is authorized for the use of a warehouse keeper whose main business is the storage of
goods placed under the customs warehousing procedure by other traders (known as depositors).

A private warehouse is for the storage of goods placed under the customs warehousing procedure by an
individual trader who is authorized as the warehouse keeper. The warehouse keeper need not necessarily own
the goods but must be the depositor.

A type E warehouse requires HMRC’s approval of a computerised accounting system, rather than of
physical premises, Goods imported under type E arrangements can be stored free of duty and VAT at any
nominated location with HMRC’s control being systems-audit-based. Movement of warehoused goods
between nominated locations may be made without duty and VAT payments and without specific customs
documentation.

Of the 6 different types of customs warehouse provided for in EU legislation, classified as A-F, only types A,
C, Dand E are available in the UK.

For details regarding different types of customs warehouses and conditions which apply for the placement of

the goods in the different types of the customs warehouses, please refer to HMRC’s Public Notice 3001 and
guidance at hitps://tinyurl.com/2xcSmxye

‘The United Kingdom | 647

--- Page 148 ---
When the goods are released from the warehouse for free circulation, duties and VAT become due, A customer
who declares the goods into free circulation becomes responsible for the payment of the VAT.

The release of goods from a bonded warehouse regime does not constitute an importation if the goods are
subsequently placed under another customs regime.

‘The sale of goods while still in bond is not subject to VAT. Evidence that the sales took place in the bonded
warehouse should be kept in the files (ice. list of the transactions from the warehouse keeper, customs
documents, the agreements, the invoices, ete.).

7.3 Option to taxation

It is possible to opt to tax transactions involving an interest in land and also limited supplies in relation to
gold (which are limited and not covered further). In relation to land and property an option to tax will be
ineffective if the property is used or intended to be used for:

+ a dwelling or a number of dwellings or solely for a ‘relevant residential’ purpose;

+ converting buildings for use as dwellings or solely for a relevant residential purpose. This block is subject
to the customer serving a form VAT1614D upon the supplier at the right time;

+ apitch for a residential caravan;

+ a building or a part of a building intended for use solely for a ‘relevant charitable’ purpose, other than
as an office, again subject to the vendor's being given a certificate that the building is to be used for that
purpose; and

+ facilities for the mooring of a residential houseboat.

In addition, the option to tax is disapplied in specific circumstances:

+ supplies of land to ‘do it yourself” (DIY) house builders + 0 someone who is building his/her own home
(i.e. not an economic activity);

+ supplies of land to housing associations where the intention is to build a dwelling or dwellings, or a
building or buildings to be used solely for a relevant residential purpose. Subject to the service of form
VATI614G on the vendor; and

+ ifthe transaction is with a connected party which is not able to deduct all of the VAT it incurs (is partly or
wholly exempt), with limited exceptions (‘the anti-avoidance measure’ ).

The conditions under which the anti-avoidance measure applies are complex, but three basic conditions must
apply:

+ the land or building concerned is or will be a capital item in the hands of the supplier. There are various
capital items related to immovable property but they all have one thing in common, they must have cost
GBP 250,000 (approximately EUR 295,000) plus VAT or more;

+ _ itis intended or expected that during the adjustment period for the capital item (10 years from when it
became a capital item) that the person occupying the immovable property will use it for a purpose for
which input tax is not (wholly or partly) deductible; and

+ the occupier is, or will be, the grantor, the person financing the development, or any person connected
with either the grantor or of the financier.

Opting to tax is a 3-part process,

1. The first part is to determine whether or not the business needs the permission of HMRC to opt to tax
using a form VATI614H, Permission is only usually required where the option relates to immovable
property which is already in the business’s ownership, and the process is all about ensuring that the
business is able to deduct only the right amount of VAT already incurred on the property (or to be
incurred). Sometimes permission is automatic. On other occasions, an application must be made to
HMRC for permission to elect. An election can only be effective once permission has been granted, The
tests are complicated and this is an aspect where professional advice needs to be obtained.

648 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 149 ---
2, The second part is that the business decides to opt to tax (please note that if permission is required, this
decision still needs to be made and the business is not bound by the permission in its application process).
it will decide the effective date of the option which can only be a current or prospective date. It is wise to
keep a current record of this decision. A company may do this, for example, by using the board minutes.

3. The third part of the process is to notify the option. This would normally be on form VATI614A (there
are other forms for different circumstances). The form can be completed and submitted online, or it can
be printed, completed and then submitted by surface mail, email or fax. An election can be notified by
the taxable person or an agent acting for the taxable person. Where an agent is used, the agent must hold
signed written authority from the taxable person stating which immovable property is subject to the
election and the effective date. The written authority must be submitted with the option to tax notification.
Always obtain some proof that the notification has been submitted. For example, if notified by surface
mail, send it ‘signed for’.

Except where the transaction is intended to be a transfer of a going concern a notification of an option to tax
must normally be submitted within 30 days of the effective date of the option. If the application is made after
this time it will only be effective at the discretion of HMRC, which is strictly applied.

HMRC acknowledgments of receipt of a notification are now no longer issued unless the notification is made
by email in which case a standard reply is sent, which does not mean that the option to tax is effective or
correct.

8. Liability

81 Reverse charges
In the UK there are several reverse charges as outlined below.

8.1.1 General Reverse charge — non-resident suppliers

‘The VAT liability of B2B supplies of the vast majority of services will be shifted to the UK customer
receiving the service. Ideally the invoice should refer to the application of the reverse charge.

For place of supply of services purposes, ‘B2B supplies’ means not only supplies to businesses whose
activities are wholly of a business nature but also supplies to entities which have both business and non-
business activities such as charities and government departments.

The reverse charge can cause otherwise unregistered organisations to become liable to VAT registration. If
anon UK supplier performs a service for a UK taxable person that is not registered, in principle the reverse
charge applies. The UK person/organisation receiving the services should then include the value of those
services when determining whether it has exceeded the UK’s VAT registration threshold of GBP 90,000
(approximately EUR 108,290).

‘The extension to the reverse charge applies if the customer has a UK VAT ID and receives B2B supplies of the
following services which are treated as supplied in the UK even where the supplier belongs outside the UK:

+ services relating to land and property such as legal or design fees;

+ services supplied where performed;

* passenger transport;

+ services covered by the additional rules for hired goods (including hired means of transport),
telecommunications services, radio and television broadcasting services; and

+ services covered by the additional rules for electronically supplied services.

Where a non-UK organisation owns property in the UK and uses it to make supplies that are VATable, either
by being zero-rated (exempt with credit - such as sales of new housing) or standard-rated (by way of new
commercial buildings or under an option to tax) then a UK VAT registration would be required.

‘The United Kingdom | 649

--- Page 150 ---
The reverse charge mechanism applies on practically all services provided by non-resident suppliers if the
recipient is established in the UK and is registered or liable to be registered for UK VAT. The reverse charge
can also apply, even if the non-resident supplier is registered for VAT in the UK.

‘There is no reverse charge mechanism for domestic supplies of goods by non-resident suppliers in the UK.
Post-Brexit B2C suppliers of goods are likely to be liable to register for UK VAT. The rules for B2B supplies
will depend on the place of supply and who acts as importer (see also paragraph 12.5 for NI).

Supplies of goods incorporating assembly or installation can only be subject to reverse charge. if the supplier
is making a one-off supply (see paragraph 3.2).

8.1.2 Specific domestic reverse charges

8.1.2.1 Supplies of natural gas and electricity
The reverse charge applies where:

+ the supply takes place in the UK;
+ the supplier is located outside the UK; and
+ the customer is registered for VAT in the UK.

Where this is the case, the UK customer is required to account for the VAT under the reverse charge
mechanism.

8.1.2.2 Domestic Reverse Charge for construction services

A reverse charge applies where a qualifying supply of construction services and associated goods is made.
VAT is only charged where the recipient is either an end user or not registered for both UK VAT and CIS
(Construction Industry Scheme).

The reverse charge upplies on the following services:

+ constructing, altering, repairing, extending, demolishing or dismantling buildings or structures (whether
permanent or noi), including offshore installation services;

+ constructing, altering, repairing, extending, demolishing of any works forming, or planned to form, part of
the land, including (in particular) walls, roadworks, power lines, electronic communications equipment,
aircraft runways, railways, inland waterways, docks and harbours, pipelines, reservoirs, water mains,
wells, sewers, industrial plant and installations for purposes of land drainage, coast protection or defence;

+ installing heating, lighting, air-conditioning, ventilation, power supply, drainage, sanitation, water supply
or fire protection systems in any building or structure;

+ internal cleaning of buildings and structures, so far as carried out in the course of their construction,
alteration, repair, extension or restoration;

+ painting or decorating the inside or the external surfaces of any building or structure; and

+ services which form an integral part of, or are part of the preparation or completion of the services described
above - including site clearance, earth-moving, excavation, tunnelling and boring, laying of foundations,
erection of scaffolding, site restoration, landscaping and the provision of roadways and other access works.

8.1.2.3 Other reverse charge provisions
On the following supplies and/or services domestic reverse charge rules apply:

+ mobile telephones;

+ computer chips;

+ investment gold and gold products of purity higher than 23.5%; and
+ emissions allowances.

A supplier should refer to the reverse charge and the amount of VAT due under the reverse charge clearly
shown. Suggested wording includes “Customer to pay output tax of GBP X to HMRC” or “UK customer to
pay O/T of GBP X to HMRC”.

In addition to the VAT return, these domestic reverse charges must be reported on a special reverse charge
sales list.

650 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 151 ---
8.2 Joint and several liability

‘There are several] situations where joint and several liability for UK VAT can arise.

+ Joint and several liability notices may be issued to an individual involved with insolvent companies that
have unpaid tax debts, which would lead to the individual being wholly responsible for the debt, if the
company no longer exists.

+ All members of a VAT group registration are jointly and severally liable for any VAT due from the group's
representative member,

+ Under anti-avoidance provisions, joint and several liability applies under which the customer can be held
liable to pay the VAT, that should have been paid by the seller on a supply of goods and services where
there is widespread fraud, that commonly involves missing trader VAT fraud.

+ On-line marketplaces can be held jointly and severally liable for the VAT due on sales by an overseas
business, that is not established in the UK and which sells goods in the UK via their website.

9, Recovery of input VAT

9.1 Non-recoverable input VAT

Any input VAT claim must be supported by a proper VAT invoice. Claims not made on the return covering the
date of supply are treated as errors and may need to be disclosed to HMRC before being reclaimed.

Input VAT is not recoverable in case of:

* goods and services not used for a trader’s business;

+ goods and services obtained before VAT registration, with some exceptions;

+ acar, including fitted accessories and delivery charges, except where used wholly for business purposes
(e.g. a taxi) in which case proof of business only use will be required;

+ business entertainment expenses, except in respect of overseas customers;

* certain articles that are installed in buildings that a developer sells or leases at the zero rate;

+ purchases that fall within the tour operators’ margin scheme;

* goods sold to a trader under one of the VAT second-hand schemes;

* assets of a business transferred to a trader as a going concern; and

* exempt supplies.

9.2 Excess input VAT

Where input tax exceeds output tax, it is refunded following submission of the period end VAT return
normally by bank transfer to the bank account recorded on the VAT registration but if none then by a GBP
payable order. If repayments of VAT are received regularly, there is a choice to improve cash-flow by
submitting monthly VAT returns so the VAT can be reclaimed more quickly.

HMRC usually process VAT repayments within 30 days. If it fails to do so, for VAT periods starting on or
before 31 December 2022 a 5% repayment supplement may be payable. For later periods if HMRC delay the
refund you may be entitled to repayment interest which is paid at the Bank of England base rate minus 1%,
with a minimum rate of 0.5%. However, the 30 day clock stops whenever HMRC commences reasonable
enquiries. No repayment supplement is payable if the return has been submitted late.

9.3 Capital goods - adjustment periods
‘The UK adjustment periods are as follows:

+ land and buildings - 10 years

+ refurbishment of buildings - 10 years

+ civil engineering works - 10 years

* computers and computer equipment - 5 years
+ boats and aircraft - 5 years.

‘The United Kingdom | 651

--- Page 152 ---
The UK does not have an adjustment period for services. Adjustment can, however, be required under the
partial exemption regime, where initial deduction is significantly different in the next VAT year.

Adjustment is also required where the intention of a taxpayer or the taxable use to which the input tax is
related changes. E.g., if input tax was intended to be used solely for making taxable supplies, and is instead
used for making exempt supplies or for a non-business purpose, then the input tax claim must be reversed.
Similarly input VAT can be claimed at a later date, where the intended exempt use did not occur and the costs
were instead put to a VATable use. The adjustment under these claw-back or pay-back provisions apply to
costs incurred over the last 6-years.

9.4 VAT refund for EU taxable persons

Non-UK businesses can reclaim VAT paid in the UK, provided that they:

+ are not registered, nor liable or eligible to be registered for UK VAT in the UK; and

+ have no place of business or other residence in the UK and do not make any supplies in the UK (other
than transport services related to the international carriage of goods, or services, where VAT is payable by
the person in the UK to whom the supply is made).

Itis a condition of the scheme that the claimant’s own country allows similar concessions to UK traders in
respect of its own turnover taxes and would not refuse to allow UK taxable persons to use it. Claims should
be made on a VAT 65A hittps://tinyurl.com/ySjenSre must be submitted, along with other documents as
outlined below, to:

The claimant can make a refund claim himself or he can use an agent. To be able to act for him and receive
money on his behalf, the agent will need either a power of attorney or a letter of authority.

Each claimant must also provide a certificate from the official authority in his own country which will be
valid for 12 months and will cover any applications made during that year. Once the certificate has expired,
claimant needs to send a new one with his next application.

The certificate should show that he is registered for business purposes in that country for which a VAT 6A
may be used- htips://tinyurl.com/2s39c4wd

The certificate must contain:

+ claimant’s own name and address;

+ if invoices are made out in claimant’s company’s trading style, the certificate must also show this; and
+ claimant’s business registration number.

HMRC expectto receive an original certificate, a photocopy is notacceptable. However they will accept electronic
certificates of status (e-CoS) if they’re issued by the official authority in the claimants own country, and that
official authority has an online validation system that HMRC can access to verify the certificate. HMRC cannot
accept ¢-Co$ electronically so in this case a copy of the e-CoS should accompany the paper refund application.
The claim - there is no limit to the maximum amount which can be claimed however the UK has set the
following minimum limits:

652 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 153 ---
+ GBP 130 (approximately EUR 150) where the claim is for less than a calendar year, but not less than
three calendar months; and
+ GBP 16 (approximately EUR 18,50) where the claim is fora calendar year, or the remainder ofa calendar year.

Deadlines

The refund must be claimed no later than 6 months after the end of the prescribed year when the VAT was
charged. The prescribed year runs from | July to 30 June, so the claim must be made by 31 December. If the
deadline is missed, it is not possible to make a claim for that year. All applications are dealt with on a ‘first
come, first served’ basis and HMRC strictly apply the time limit.

Other details:

+ Ifthe claimant has arranged for goods to be imported into the UK, provided there is no other VAT relief
available at import, the import VAT due can be reclaimed. Claims cannot be made if the import results in
the claimant being liable to register for UK VAT.

+ It is possible to include items missed on earlier claims so long as they relate to VAT charged in the
calendar year of the claim.

+ The claim cannot be for a period of:

- more than the prescribed year; or
- less than 3 months - unless that is all that is left of the prescribed year.

+ HMRC must be sent all the original invoices for all the VAT that is reclaimed.

+ HMRC will normally pay within 6 months of receiving the claim, unless the claimant needs to provide
more information.

Itis not possible to claim:

* amounts that relate to non-business costs but if a supply covers both business and non-business use, VAT
can be reclaimed on the business element of the supply:

+ any supply used or to be used to make a supply in the United Kingdom;

* amounts of VAT that have been incorrectly invoiced;

+ the supply or importation of most ordinary business cars, only $0% of the VAT incurred on the hire of
lease of a car for mixed business and private purposes is allowed;

* certain second-hand goods, such as cars and antiques for which no tax invoice will be issued;

+ business entertainment or hospitality expenses, as an exception, VAT on entertainment for overseas
customers may be reclaimed but only if it is of a very basic nature;

+ export of goods, but these will be zero-rated, provided the supplier has the necessary evidence

* goods and services, such as hotel accommodation, you have bought for resale which are for the direct
benefit of travellers; and

* any supply used or to be used to make an exempt supply outside the United Kingdom, (for this purpose
an exempt supply is a supply described as exempt in Schedule 9 to the VAT Act 1994, whether or not the
place of the supply is in the UK).

If HMRC refuses the application and the claimant does not agree with the decision to refuse the application, it
is possible to:

+ ask for the decision to be reviewed by an HMRC officer not previously involved in the matter; or

* appeal to an independent tribunal.

Ifthe claimant opts fora review, he can still appeal to the tribunal after the review has finished. Ifhe wants areview
he should write to the address given in the decision letter within 30 days of the date of the letter, giving his reasons
why he does not agree with the decision. If the dispute results in a payment to HMRC being called for, HMRC will
not take any action to collect the disputed tax while the review of the decision is being carried out. Ifa claimant
wants to appeal to the tribunal then this should be submitted within 30 days of the date of the decision letter.
