10. Administrative obligations

10.1 Invoicing

10.1.1 Invoice content

A valid VAT invoice must contain:

+ aunique invoice number in a consecutive sequence;

+ the invoice date (as in the date of issue);

+ the supplier’s name or trading name, and address;

+ the supplier's VAT number;

+ the customer’s name or trading name and address;

+ adescription sufficient to identify the nature of the goods or services supplied;

+ the time of supply (tax point), which can be different from the date of the invoice;

+ where a VAT invoice relates in whole or part to a supply where the person supplied is liable to pay the tax,
the reference “reverse charge”;
+ — for self-billed supplies, the reference “self-billing”;
+ for each different type of item listed on the invoice, the following must be shown:
+ the unit price or rate, excluding VAT;
+ the rate of VAT that applies to what’s being sold;
* the rate of any cash discount;
+ total amount payable, excluding VAT;
+ the quantity of goods or the extent of the services; and
+ the total amount of VAT charged;
+ if a VAT invoice includes zero rated or exempt goods or services, the following must be shown:
+ that there is no VAT payable on those goods or services; and
+ the total of those values separately;
+ the information necessary to determine whether there is a new means of transport; and
+ an indication of the fact that a margin scheme has been applied as well as an indication why the margin
scheme is applied, e.g.:
+ margin scheme for second-hand goods;
+ margin scheme for works of art;
+ margin scheme for collector's items and antiques; or
+ tour operator margin scheme.

Please note that even where the transaction has been concluded in a different currency the invoice must show
the GBP equivalent value.

It is possible to issue the invoice in any language. However, the issuer must provide an English translation of
any invoice within 30 days if asked to do so by a VAT Auditor.

10.1.2 Time limits

Where a registered taxable person makes a taxable supply in the UK, to a taxable person, it is required to
provide such persons with a VAT invoice within 30 days of the time when the supply is treated as taking place
or within such longer period as HMRC may allow.

For supplies of services lasting more than | calendar year (i.¢. so-called continuous services) a tax invoice
must be provided by the January following the 31 December of each year when the services are provided.

654 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 155 ---
10.1.3 Currency
Any currency may be used on an invoice, but where VAT is shown, the VAT amount must also be converted
into GBP and shown on the face of the invoice. In addition, where VAT is not charged, the net amount of the
transaction must be converted into GBP, if the supplier wishes to rely upon the invoice.

10.1.4 Conversion and exchange rate
Foreign currency must be converted into GBP using either:

+ the period rate of exchange as published by HMRC;

+ the UK market selling rate at the time of the supply. The rates published in national newspapers are
acceptable; or

+ the rate a trader uses for commercial purposes, but he/she may only do so upon successful application to
HMRC. Annual rates are likely to be refused as well as any rate which varies significantly from either of
the other two options.

10.1.5 Simplified Invoice

Where the consideration for a supply does not exceed GBP 250 (approximately EUR 289) the invoice need
only contain the following:

+ the name, address and registration number of the supplier;

» the time of the supply;

+ adescription sufficient to identify the goods or services;

+ the total amount payable including VAT; and

+ for each rate of VAT the gross amount including VAT payable and the VAT rate.

10.1.6 Self-billing

While no approval is required for self-billing the conditions below must be met. The agreement entered into
between supplier and customer usually lasts for 12 months, At the end of that period, it should be reviewed
so there is evidence to show that the supplier agreed to accept the invoices issued on his behalf. However, if
a customer has a business contract with a supplier, the self-billing agreement should last until the end date of
the contract and when the contract has expired the self-billing agreement should be reviewed.

However there are conditions that must be fulfilled in order for the self-billed invoice to be valid:

* there must be an agreement at the outset between the supplier and the customer. There must be a new
agreement if the supplier transfers his business as a going concern, and both the customer and the person
who bought the business wish to continue with self-billing;

* the customer must raise self-bills for all transactions with the supplier named in the agreement for up to
twelve months ot, if there is a contract with the supplier, for the duration of that contract;

+ the self-bills must show all the details required for a full VAT invoice;

+ _ both parties must keep copies of the agreement and produce it to HMRC on request;

+ the agreement must authorize the customer to produce self-bills and show that the supplier accepts all
self-bills issued during the agreement period;

* the supplier must be VAT registered throughout the agreement period and must not issue VAT invoices for
supplies covered by the agreement;

* the customer must keep and maintain an updated list of the names, addresses and VAT ID numbers of
suppliers, who have agreed to self-billing, and this list must be made available to HMRC upon request;

+ ifa supplier’s VAT 1D number changes or he ceases to be VAT registered, he must tell his self-billing
customer immediately; and

* the words “The VAT shown is your output tax due to HM Revenue and Customs” must be on the self-
billed invoices along with a reference such as “self-billing”.

Importantly the dates and GBP values shown on any SBI issued by a customer must be used as the basis for
the supplier’s own VAT declarations.

The United Kingdom | 655

--- Page 156 ---
10.1.7 Storage of invoices

Invoices must be retained for 6 years. Copies of invoices can also be retained electronically. A taxable person
must print the document for the HMRC if a request is made.

10.1.8 Electronic invoicing

It is allowed to send and receive invoices clectronically instead of using paper invoices. There is no
requirement to inform HMRC, bul, a taxable person must make sure that its systems and procedures are
proper.

Just as with issuing paper invoices, whatever form of electronic invoicing a taxable person chooses to use, it
smust be able to:

+ make sure that invoice data is complete and accurate;

+ make sure invoices are sent out when they need to be;

+ prevent invoices from being corrupted and be alerted if problems do occur;

+ make sure it does not accidentally send out duplicate invoices, or that customers do not accidentally
process an invoice twice; and

* ensure that customers do not automatically process invoices and try to reclaim VAT when they should not,
e.g. invoices issued under a margin scheme.

Ifa taxable person issues electronic credit notes or debit notes, they should include the same information as
paper versions. They must include:

+ the same details as the original invoice they refer to; and
* — enough information to link them to the original invoice.

10.2 VAT compliance

10.2.1 VAT return period

Once a taxable person has a UK VAT registration number, it must complete a VAT return online and comply
with the UK Making Tax Digital obligations. Unless monthly VAT returns have been approved, returns are
submitted quarterly (small businesses can opt to make annual returns). Return period ends do not need to
coincide with calendar quarters by getting HMRC’s approval to use non-standard tax periods. Although
most returns are completed on a quarterly basis, a large payer (over GBP 2,300,000 (approximately EUR
2,714,350) must make monthly payments on account (‘POA’), even if it submits quarterly VAT returns,

The requirements are identical for resident companies and non-resident companies.

10.2.2 Filing of a VAT return and payment

The return should be submitted and any net VAT due paid over to HMRC by the last working day before the
7 day after the end of the month following the reporting quarter/month (*® the due date is earlier if the 7"
day falls over a weekend). A large payer making monthly POA has to submit and pay by the end of the month
following the VAT period. If it pays by direct debit (not allowed for POA registrations) the banking system
means it receives a few extra days to pay.

The submission and payment requirements are identical for resident companies and non-resident companies,

HMRC’s guide to completing online VAT returns can be downloaded at the following link:
Attps://tinyurl.com/mtbtc9xs

10.2.3. European sales listing
European sales lists for goods or servives ure nol required.

10.3 Penalties

656 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 157 ---
10.3.1 Default penalties for late registration

A penalty is due, if the application to be VAT registered is late. The penalty is based on the net tax due
between the required date and the actual late date of filing. If the authorities are notified voluntarily within 9
months, the penalty is usually 5% of the net tax due, between 9 months and 18 months late then usually 10%
of the net tax due, and usually 15% of the net tax due thereafter subject to a minimum penalty of GBP 50.
Any penalty can be appealed and HMRC may reduce or remove a penalty where there is a reasonable excuse
for failing to register at the correct time.

10.3.2 Default penalties for not filing a VAT return

The UK VAT penalty regime was overhauled for VAT returns periods starting on or after 1 January 2023,
Instead of being based purely on due dates. The new points system is based on the following framework:

Annual 2 Points

Quarterly 4 Points

Monthly 5 Points

registration.
(ii) Once one of the penalty thresholds as above has been breached, a GBP 200 penalty will fall due.
(ii) For every additional penalty point imposed a GBP 200 penalty will fall due
(iv) To reset the penalty point balance to zero the registration must have submitted all VAT return
for the previous 24 months and had a period of timely VAT return submissions. This period of
compliance is according to the return frequency so for:
+ Annual submissions + he period is 24 months
+ Quarterly submissions - the period is 12 months
* Monthly submissions - the period is 6 months
wv) If the penalty threshold has not been breached, each point expires 24 months after issue

10.3.4 Default penalties on VAT assessments when VAT is not reported correctly
The penalty system is based on two main concepts:

+ whether the taxable person has exercised ‘reasonable care’ within its tax accounting; and
+ whether the taxable person has made an ‘unprompted disclosure’.

The penalty system has two tariffs - one for unprompted disclosures and a higher one for prompted
disclosures. The penalties are all based on the amount of tax involved in an error, where HMRC gets paid too
little tax. The penalty is due even if HMRC does not lose out. E.g. if a business fails to charge VAT, and its
customer would have been entitled to reclaim all of the VAT that should have been charged, a penalty is still
due. The penalties can be mitigated by HMRC to reflect the behaviour and circumstances that led to the error.

HMRC expects that every taxable person will keep records that allow the taxable person to provide a
complete and accurate return. HMRC also expects the taxable person to check with its agent, or HMRC, to
confirm the correct position, if there is uncertainty. However, HMRC says that ‘reasonable care’ is different
according to each taxable person’s circumstances and abilities. E.g. a corporation with an in-house tax
department will find it much more difficult to justify a mistake was made after taking reasonable care, than a
small business which does not have the same in-house tax management capability.

10.3.5 Unprompted disclosure tariff (if the error is disclosed voluntarily)

Reason for error Minimum penalty |Maximum penalty
Careless (the taxable person fails to take reasonable care) 0% 30%

Deliberate (the taxable person knowingly sends HMRC an 20% 70%

incorrect document)

Deliberate and concealed (the taxable person knowingly 30% 100%

sends HMRC an incorrect document and tries to conceal the

inaccuracy)

10.3.6 Prompted Disclosure Tariff (e.g. a tax auditor has begun asking questions)

Reason for error imum penalty | Maximum penalty

Careless (the taxable person fails to take reasonable care) 30%
Deliberate (the taxable person knowingly sends HMRC an 70%
incorrect document)

Deliberate and concealed (the taxable person knowingly 50% 100%
sends HMRC an incorrect document and tries to conceal the

inaccuracy)

10.3.7 VAT voluntary disclosures made on VAT returns

Depending on the value of the error it is possible lo adjust for errors on the VAT return, whereas larger errors
must be dealt with by way of voluntary disclosure. Adjustment in these cases will usually be dealt with by
HMRC issuing a VAT assessment,

Even where an error is below the prescribed disclosure limit, penalties might be mitigated by choosing to
make a disclosure to HMRC towards showing that reasonable care was taken when making the original
declaration.

10.3.8 Suspended penalties

The penalty regime provides for penalties to be suspended, typically for periods of | 2 - years. Suspended
penalties are intended to encourage positive behaviours in a taxable person, with a view to preventing future
careless inaccuracies.

All penalties can only be avoided, if the taxable person is able to successfully argue that there was a
‘reasonable excuse’ for the late return or payment.

658 | The United Kingdom

--- Page 159 ---
10.4 Intrastat

UK Intrastat obligations are now limited to certain transactions involving Northern Ireland. With effect from
01 January 2021 monthly Intrastat returns need only be submitted to HMRC, if in any calendar year (from |
January to 31 December), your business either:

+ receives more than GBP 500,000 (approximately EUR 590,000) worth of goods from the EU into
Northern Ireland

+ moves more than GBP 250,000 (approximately EUR 295,000) worth of goods to the EU from Northern
Ireland

You do not need to use Intrastat for goods you move between Great Britain (England, Wales and Scotland)
and the EU, or between Great Britain and Northern Ireland. These Intrastat obligations apply irrespective of
where the customer or supplier’s business is located.

Any declarations that are required must be submitted no later than the 21° day of the month following the
period end. Periods are normally calendar months but, by concession, taxable persons approved to submit
VAT returns for non-standard accounting periods may also submit Intrastat returns for the same periods.
Intrastat returns can now only be submitted on online or using specialist software. It is possible to perform
data uploads. Amendments can also be made online,

10.5 Additional Compliance Requirements

10.5.1 Making Tax Digital
All UK VAT registrations must submit their VAT returns using software and keep their VAT records digitally,
basically accounting for VAT using a fully digital accounting journey. This is often achieved by using an
accounting package with an integrated VAT filer. Non-UK businesses often use a UK VAT Agent to prepare
and file VAT returns via the agent’s own MTD compliant system.

10.5.2 ‘Tax assessment period

The tax assessment period in the UK is 4 years from the end of the tax period concerned. In the case of fraud,
the tax assessment period is 20 years. If'a business does not register on time, HMRC can take the registration
date back to the start of the business provided this was after 1973 and collect back-VAT on alll sales made.

10.5.3 Retention of business records

HMRC require all VAT registered business to retain all business records for a period of six-years. This includes
not only VAT accounting records but also bank statements, agreements, contracts, export evidence etc.

10.5.4 Post-Brexit consequences

Post-Brexit, where UK sellers supply electronic, broadcasting or telecoms services to EU consumers, they
will have register for Non-Union OSS in any EU state and thereby account for EU VAT on all B2C supplies
of digital services.

EU organisations can no longer account for UK VAT via an EU MOSS return and therefore need to register
for UK VAT to account for UK VAT on sales of digital services to UK based consumers.

Any non-EU organisation that previously paid EU VAT via a UK MOSS registration will have to re-register
for Non-Union OSS in the EU and separately register for UK VAT.

10.5.5 Northern Ireland Protocol

Brexit has led to a two-tier system to operate around Northern Ireland (’NI’). UK VAT will be due on all
supplies made to NI from England, Scotland and Wales and vice versa. This respects the fact that NI is part of
the UK,

However the same pre-Brexit rules will continue to apply to EU movements of goods involving NI while

at the same time NI remains part of the UK’s VAT system. This table might help distinguish treatment in
different scenarios:

The United Kingdom | 659

--- Page 160 ---
Goods moving from/to VAT treatment
GB to EU Importation in the Member State & UK export
EU to GB ihe Export from the Member State & import into UK

NI to BU. Intra-Community transaction

EU to NI Intra~Community transaction

The new ‘XI’ VAT and EORIJ numbers should now be used on invoices, statistical reporting and customs
documents for:

+ goods located in NI at the time of sale;
+ goods supplied B2B into NI by VAT-registered EU businesses; or
+ the sale or movement goods from NI to FU.

Intra-EU rules and simplifications, such as triangulation and ‘call off stock’, are not available for movements
of goods involving Great Britain, however these simplifications continue to be available for movements of
goods involving EU member states and NI or where the business involved is identified as moving goods in,
from, or to, NI.

B2C NI: The distance selling rules still apply to businesses moving goods from Northern Ireland to the EU
and vice versa -$ ee https./hinyurl.com/b3tu3jsb. Sales of goods by overseas sellers fall under special rules
which are broadly the same as those previously in place for the rest of the UK. The main difference is that
responsibility for charging and declaring VAT on goods held in NI, by overseas sellers that are sold B2C via
an Online Marketplace (OMP) to NI consumers will rest with the sellers,

The Windsor Framework was agreed on 27 February 2023 between the UK and EU intended to roll out a new
set of arrangements to restore the smooth flow of trade within the UK internal market and also EU trade with
NI. From 30 September 2024 the new UK Internal Market Scheme (UKIMS) saw the full ‘green lane’ taking
effect for authorised traders allowing them to move goods between mainland UK and NI, without using full
Customs procedures.

From 1 January 2025 new rules were introduced for medicines ensuring the same labelling, packaging,
licensing etc will apply across mainland UK and NI. Suppliers will no longer need to apply for separate
licences to market the same medicines across the whole UK.

Further changes under the framework are also planned for parcels and freight movements from 31 March
2025 enabling eligible goods moving from a business in mainland UK to a business in Northern Ireland
that are for sale to, or final use by, end consumers located in the UK, to move without the need for a full
international customs declaration and without incurring duty under the UK IMS. Current guidance can be
found here:

https://tinyurl.com/mr3z2veh.
